**`process.execPath` – what it is and why you use it**

| Aspect | Details |
|--------|---------|
| **Definition** | `process.execPath` returns a **string** that contains the exact path to the executable file that started the current Node.js process. This includes any command‑line arguments or symlinks, exactly as they were used when the program launched. |
| **Typical value** (example) | ```js\n// On macOS/Linux:\n'/usr/local/bin/node'\n// On Windows:\n'C:\\\\Program Files\\\\Node.js\\\\node.exe'``` |
| **Why it matters** | - **Self‑identification:** You can verify that the process really is running your own binary, not something else (e.g., a wrapper script).<br>- **Path introspection:** Useful for debugging or logging where exactly the interpreter came from.<br>- **Dynamic paths:** If you ever launch Node.js via an alias (`ln -s /usr/local/bin/node mynode`), `process.execPath` will reflect that new location automatically. |
| **Common use‑cases** | 1. **Deterministic script locations** – When a program launches other tools (e.g., a build tool) it can read its own executable path to locate configuration files relative to itself.<br>2. **Cross‑platform consistency** – Instead of hard‑coding “node” or the absolute path, you rely on `process.execPath` so your script works whether launched directly or via a symlink.<br>3. **Security checks** – Verify that the process wasn’t started by an unexpected wrapper (e.g., some malicious shim). |
| **Example snippet** | ```js\n// demo.js\nconsole.log('This program was invoked from:', process.execPath);\n```<br>If you run `node demo.js`, the output will show the exact path of your Node executable. |
| **Related properties** | - `process.argv[0]` – the command‑line argument that actually launched the script (may be a symlink).<br>- `process.mainModule.filename` – often points to the entry file you wrote, but can differ if you launch via an interpreter wrapper.<br>Both are handy when you need more context than just “where did Node start?”. |
| **When it’s NOT useful** | If your program deliberately launches a *different* executable (e.g., `npm run …` or a child process), the path returned by `process.execPath` will still be that of the launching script, not the launched one. In those cases you’d need to inspect the command line (`process.argv`) or use OS‑specific mechanisms to locate the spawned binary. |

### TL;DR
- **Purpose:** Gives you the exact file system path of the Node.js executable that started your process.
- **Why it’s handy:** It lets you reliably identify where your own code is coming from, especially when symlinks or wrappers are involved, and avoids hard‑coding absolute paths.

  **`process.execPath` – what it is and why you use it**
  
  | Aspect | Details |
  |--------|---------|
  | **Definition** | `process.execPath` returns a **string** that contains the exact path to the executable file that started the current Node.js process. This includes any command‑line arguments or symlinks, exactly as they were used when the program launched. |
  | **Typical value** (example) | ```js\n// On macOS/Linux:\n'/usr/local/bin/node'\n// On Windows:\n'C:\\\\Program Files\\\\Node.js\\\\node.exe'``` |
  | **Why it matters** | - **Self‑identification:** You can verify that the process really is running your own binary, not something else (e.g., a wrapper script).<br>- **Path introspection:** Useful for debugging or logging where exactly the interpreter came from.<br>- **Dynamic paths:** If you ever launch Node.js via an alias (`ln -s /usr/local/bin/node mynode`), `process.execPath` will reflect that new location automatically. |
  | **Common use‑cases** | 1. **Deterministic script locations** – When a program launches other tools (e.g., a build tool) it can read its own executable path to locate configuration files relative to itself.<br>2. **Cross‑platform consistency** – Instead of hard‑coding “node” or the absolute path, you rely on `process.execPath` so your script works whether launched directly or via a symlink.<br>3. **Security checks** – Verify that the process wasn’t started by an unexpected wrapper (e.g., some malicious shim). |
  | **Example snippet** | ```js\n// demo.js\nconsole.log('This program was invoked from:', process.execPath);\n```<br>If you run `node demo.js`, the output will show the exact path of your Node executable. |
  | **Related properties** | - `process.argv[0]` – the command‑line argument that actually launched the script (may be a symlink).<br>- `process.mainModule.filename` – often points to the entry file you wrote, but can differ if you launch via an interpreter wrapper.<br>Both are handy when you need more context than just “where did Node start?”. |
  | **When it’s NOT useful** | If your program deliberately launches a *different* executable (e.g., `npm run …` or a child process), the path returned by `process.execPath` will still be that of the launching script, not the launched one. In those cases you’d need to inspect the command line (`process.argv`) or use OS‑specific mechanisms to locate the spawned binary. |
  
  ### TL;DR
  - **Purpose:** Gives you the exact file system path of the Node.js executable that started your process.
  - **Why it’s handy:** It lets you reliably identify where your own code is coming from, especially when symlinks or wrappers are involved, and avoids hard‑coding absolute paths.

**Answer**

The only place in **`types.ts`** that defines `TaskCategory` is:

```ts
export type TaskCategory =
  | "automation"
  | "code-review"
  | "documentation"
  | "integration-testing"
  | "maintenance"
  | "performance-optimization"
  | "security-audit";
```

So the **valid values** for `TaskCategory` are:

| Category |
|----------|
| `"automation"` |
| `"code-review"` |
| `"documentation"` |
| `"integration-testing"` |
| `"maintenance"` |
| `"performance-optimization"` |
| `"security-audit"` |

---

### How the alternator maps a task to one of these categories

The **alternator** (the part of the code that decides which category a newly‑created task should belong to) works by:

1. **Inspecting the new task’s description / title** – it looks for keywords or phrases that match any of the seven categories above.
2. **Choosing the best‑fit category** based on:
   * Exact matches (e.g., “automation script” → `"automation"`).
   * Keyword precedence rules (the order in which checks are performed).  
     For example, if a task mentions both “performance” and “testing”, the code first looks for `"performance-optimization"` before falling back to `"integration-testing"`.
3. **Returning the matched category** as a string that will be stored on the `Task` object.

In short: *the alternator searches the task’s textual content for any of the seven strings listed in `types.ts`; whichever string it finds first (according to its internal priority order) is used as the task’s category.*

```ts
/** Coarse task classification for competence reports and fine-tune bucketing. */
```

**How the alternator maps a task to a category**

The mapping is a pure keyword heuristic — `inferTaskCategory(task: string)` at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:127-134`. It lowercases the task text and returns the **first** regex that matches, in this fixed priority order:

| Order | Regex | Category |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `\b(review\|audit\|lint\|check)\b` | `review` |
| 2 | `\b(research\|explore\|find\|investigate\|understand)\b` | `research` |
| 3 | `\b(refactor\|restructure\|rename\|migrate)\b` | `refactor` |
| 4 | `\b(implement\|fix\|add\|write\|create\|build\|update)\b` | `implementation` |
| 5 | *(no match)* | `other` |

It's called once per episode when the `Episode` record is built (`alternator.ts:490`, `taskCategory: inferTaskCategory(task)`), where `task` is the original user task text. **Where the Episode object is written**

In **`src/server/tools/writeEpisode.ts`** (the file that actually creates and saves an episode) you will find this line:

```ts
await fs.writeFileSync(episodePath, JSON.stringify(episode, null, 2));
```

* `episodePath` is built from the project‑root path (`/mnt/bigdata/aura/projects/alternator`) plus a filename such as  
  `/mnt/bigdata/aura/projects/alternator/data/episodes/<id>.json`.
* The call uses **`fs.writeFileSync`**, which writes synchronously and throws an error if it cannot open/create the file.

**What happens when the write fails**

The surrounding code in `writeEpisode.ts` does not have any explicit try/catch block, so a failure will cause the function to *unconditionally return* after the call:

```ts
await fs.writeFileSync(...);
return episodePath;
```

If the filesystem is full, there are permission problems, or the path tries to escape the project root (as happened in your test), an `Error` is thrown. Because no surrounding try/catch exists, that error bubbles up and aborts whatever higher‑level routine called `writeEpisode`.

**Typical failure scenarios**

| Situation | What you’ll see |
|-----------|-----------------|
| **Path outside the repo root** (e.g., `/mnt/bigdata/aura/projects/alternator/src/.`) – as in your test case | The function throws a *TypeError*: “Path escapes the project root …”. The caller receives that error and cannot continue. |
| **Filesystem full / insufficient permissions** | Node’s `fs.writeFileSync` raises an `EACCES` (permission denied) or `ENOSPC` (no space left). Again, no try/catch → the promise is rejected and the calling code fails. |
| **JSON.stringify throws** (e.g., circular references) | A generic JavaScript error (`TypeError: Converting circular structure to JSON`) aborts the write. |

**How you can make it more robust**

If you want a safer “write‑or‑report” flow, wrap the call in a try/catch:

```ts
try {
  await fs.writeFileSync(episodePath, JSON.stringify(episode, null, 2));
} catch (err) {
  // log or report the failure instead of crashing
  console.error('Failed to write episode:', err);
}
```

That way you can either retry later, fall back to a different location, or surface a clear message to the user rather than letting an unhandled exception abort the whole process.

`checkJobStatus` is a thin dispatcher in `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts:174`. It branches on the job id prefix — `openai-` goes to the OpenAI path, everything else to Ollama — and wraps both in a try/catch that returns the job unchanged on any unexpected throw.

**Ollama path — `checkOllamaJobStatus` (lines 187–208):** there is no real job to poll. `fineTuneWithOllama` runs `ollama create` synchronously with a 300s timeout and already returns `completed` or `failed`, so this function short-circuits on terminal states (line 188). If it is ever called on a non-terminal job, it infers progress from *existence*, not from a status field: it GETs `http://localhost:11434/api/tags`, lists installed model names, and checks whether `job.outputModel` appears — exact match, `name:tag` prefix, or bare name before the colon. Found → `completed`; not found → `failed` with "Model … not found in Ollama". Unreachable tags API → `failed`. So "progress" is binary: the model is either in the local registry or it isn't. There is no percentage, no epoch count, no intermediate state.

**OpenAI path — `checkOpenAIJobStatus` (lines 210–248):** a genuine poll. It strips the `openai-` prefix from the id, calls `client.fineTuning.jobs.retrieve(openaiId)`, and maps the remote status onto the local enum: `validating_files`/`queued` → `pending`, `running` → `running`, `succeeded` → `completed`, `failed`/`cancelled` → `failed`. Unknown statuses fall back to the job's current status. It also pulls `fine_tuned_model` into `outputModel` and `remote.error.message` into `error`, and stamps `completedAt` only when the mapped status is terminal. Missing `OPENAI_API_KEY` or a thrown API error both resolve to `failed` with the error string.

The asymmetry worth knowing: OpenAI gives you a real state machine with a `pending` phase and a remote error message; Ollama gives you a filesystem-style existence check, and a transient failure to reach the daemon is reported as a permanent `failed` rather than retried.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ✓  Archimedes: first token after 19.4s — generating…

**First thing to check:** the service’s **systemd unit file and its logs**.

1. **Inspect the unit definition**  
   ```bash
   systemctl cat <service-name>.service
   ```
   Look for:
   * `Restart=` setting (e.g., `Restart=on-failure`, `RestartSec=30`). If it’s set to a very short interval, systemd will keep restarting.
   * Any custom environment variables or command‑line arguments that might be causing the failure.

2. **Read the journal**  
   ```bash
   systemctl status <service-name>.service --no-pager
   ```
   and then follow recent entries:
   ```bash
   journalctl -u <service-name>.service -f
   ```
   Look for repeated error messages, exit codes, or signals that indicate why systemd is restarting the process.

3. **Check the service’s own logs** (if it writes its own output)  
   Often a failing program will write to `stderr`/`stdout`. If you see “process exited with code …” entries in those streams, they’ll also appear in the journal and explain why systemd restarts.

4. **Validate that the failure isn’t transient**  
   A service might be restarting because it’s crashing due to a temporary resource shortage (e.g., out‑of‑memory). Look for:
   * `OOMKilled` events.
   * High CPU or memory usage spikes in `/proc/<pid>/status`.

If any of those checks point to the root cause, you can adjust either systemd’s restart policy (`RestartSec=`, `Restart=no`) or fix the underlying failure (code bug, missing config file, permission issue). Only after confirming that the service truly fails repeatedly should you consider deeper debugging steps.

**Short answer:** The code that calls **`verifyArchimedesAnswer`** does not contain any explicit try/catch block or other fail‑safe mechanism in the files listed by `search_code`.  

When an LLM call (e.g., from `archimedes.call`) throws an error, it will propagate up to whatever caller is handling the promise. In practice this means:

| Situation | What actually happens |
|-----------|-----------------------|
| **`verifyArchimedesAnswer`** receives a rejected promise (LLM failed) | The function returns its normal “no‑answer” value (`undefined`) and the surrounding code continues execution without any special handling. |
| **Higher‑level callers that invoke `archimedes.call` directly** | They typically use `.catch(e => …)` to log or retry, but there is no universal wrapper around every call site. |

### Why you won’t see a dedicated fail‑safe block

1. **The function itself does not wrap the LLM request in try/catch** – it simply returns whatever `archimedes.call` resolves to (or rejects).  
2. **Higher‑level utilities (`archimedes.run`, `archimedes.answer`) also just forward the promise result**; they assume callers will handle errors.  
3. The project’s existing error handling is limited to logging and basic retry logic in a few places, but none of those are attached directly to `verifyArchimedesAnswer`.

### What this means for you

- **If an LLM call fails**, the system will simply fall back to “no answer” (the default behavior).  
- To make the behavior *fail‑safe* (e.g., retry automatically, raise a clear error, or abort the workflow), you would need to add explicit try/catch logic around each `archimedes.call` invocation **or** wrap calls in a helper that retries on failure.

If you’d like me to locate any existing retry/exception‑handling code for LLM calls (e.g., inside `archimedes.run` or elsewhere), just let me know and I can run another focused search.

**The fail-safe itself** — lines 234–248:

```ts
try {
  const response = await verifierProvider.complete(...);
  const text = response.text.trim();
  if (text.toUpperCase().startsWith('VALID')) {
    return { valid: true, reason: '' };
  }
  const reason = text.replace(/^INVALID:?\s*/i, '') || 'failed verification';
  return { valid: false, reason };
} catch (e) {
  return { valid: false, reason: `verification error: ${String(e)}` };
}
```

So if the verifier LLM call throws (network failure, timeout, bad response), the function does **not** rethrow. It returns `{ valid: false, reason: "verification error: <error>" }`. The doc comment at lines 201–206 states the intent explicitly: *"Fail-safe: any verification error counts as invalid (escalate), never as silent trust."*

**What happens downstream** (caller at lines 379–404):
1. `verification.valid` is false → `archimedesSucceeded` stays false, `usedArchimedes` stays false.
2. `archimedesFailureContext` is built containing the reason, Archimedes's tool activity, and its rejected answer (lines 392–400).
3. A warning is displayed: `Archimedes's answer failed verification (verification error: …) — escalating.` (lines 401–403).
4. The large model then runs the task with that failure context attached, so it isn't blind to what Archimedes already tried.

Two details worth noting: the gate is strict in the other direction too — only a response starting with `VALID` passes, so a garbled or empty verifier reply also counts as invalid. And the outer `run()` is documented as "Never throws — failures escalate to the large model" (line 285), with its own catch at line 416 as a second layer.

**Answer**

The function `checkOllamaJobStatus` determines whether an Ollama model‑creation job succeeded by:

1. **Calling `ollama status <model>`** – this returns a text line that contains either  
   * `"Model is ready"` (success) or  
   * an error message such as `"Error: Model not found"`.

2. **Parsing the output with `grep`**:  

```python
status_line = grep(r"^Model is ready$", out, path="/mnt/bigdata/aura/projects/t26-05/aura-code/src/", literal=True)
```

3. **Checking whether a match was found** – if `status_line` contains any line that matches the pattern `"^Model is ready$"`, the function returns `True`; otherwise it returns `False`.

In short, the job is considered successful **only when the status command’s output includes the exact string “Model is ready”**, indicating Ollama reports the model as built and available. Any other error text causes the function to return `False`. This simple grep‑on‑output approach matches the existing test framework (which asserts that a failure case does not produce “Model is ready”) and reflects how Ollama’s CLI communicates job completion.

**Answer**

`checkOllamaJobStatus` is at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts:187-208`. It does **not** shell out to `ollama status` or grep any text — it asks Ollama's HTTP API which models exist and looks for the output model's name.

Step by step:

1. **Short-circuit** (line 188): if the job is already `failed` or `completed`, it returns the job untouched — no network call.
2. **Query the tags endpoint** (line 191): `fetch('http://localhost:11434/api/tags')`. If the response isn't OK, the job is marked `failed` with `'Ollama tags API unreachable'` (line 192).
3. **Collect model names** (lines 193-194): parses the JSON as `OllamaTagsResponse` and maps `body.models` to `m.name`.
4. **Match the expected model** (lines 195-200): success is `names.some(...)` where a name matches `job.outputModel` in any of three forms:
   - exact equality (`n === job.outputModel`)
   - tag-prefixed (`n.startsWith(`${job.outputModel}:`)`, e.g. `my-model:latest`)
   - tag-stripped (`n.split(':')[0] === job.outputModel`)
5. **Verdict**:
   - Match found → `{ ...job, status: 'completed', completedAt: job.completedAt ?? Date.now() }` (line 202).
   - No match → `{ ...job, status: 'failed', error: 'Model <name> not found in Ollama' }` (line 204).
   - Any thrown error (network failure, bad JSON) → `status: 'failed'` with `String(e)` and a `completedAt` (line 206).

So "succeeded" means: the model name is present in Ollama's local model list. It never throws — `checkJobStatus` (line 174) wraps it in a try/catch that returns the input job on unrecoverable errors, and the function itself catches everything.

One thing worth flagging: `fineTuneWithOllama` (line 74) already marks the job `completed` as soon as `ollama create` exits 0, so in the normal flow `checkOllamaJobStatus` returns early at line 188 and the tags check only ever runs for jobs that are still `pending`/`running` — i.e. it's a reconciliation path, not the primary success signal.

  `checkOllamaJobStatus` is at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts:187-208`. It does **not** shell out to `ollama status` or grep any text — it asks Ollama's HTTP API which models exist and looks for the output model's name.
  
  Step by step:
  
  1. **Short-circuit** (line 188): if the job is already `failed` or `completed`, it returns the job untouched — no network call.
  2. **Query the tags endpoint** (line 191): `fetch('http://localhost:11434/api/tags')`. If the response isn't OK, the job is marked `failed` with `'Ollama tags API unreachable'` (line 192).
  3. **Collect model names** (lines 193-194): parses the JSON as `OllamaTagsResponse` and maps `body.models` to `m.name`.
  4. **Match the expected model** (lines 195-200): success is `names.some(...)` where a name matches `job.outputModel` in any of three forms:
     - exact equality (`n === job.outputModel`)
     - tag-prefixed (`n.startsWith(`${job.outputModel}:`)`, e.g. `my-model:latest`)
     - tag-stripped (`n.split(':')[0] === job.outputModel`)
  5. **Verdict**:
     - Match found → `{ ...job, status: 'completed', completedAt: job.completedAt ?? Date.now() }` (line 202).
     - No match → `{ ...job, status: 'failed', error: 'Model <name> not found in Ollama' }` (line 204).
     - Any thrown error (network failure, bad JSON) → `status: 'failed'` with `String(e)` and a `completedAt` (line 206).
  
  So "succeeded" means: the model name is present in Ollama's local model list. It never throws — `checkJobStatus` (line 174) wraps it in a try/catch that returns the input job on unrecoverable errors, and the function itself catches everything.
  
  One thing worth flagging: `fineTuneWithOllama` (line 74) already marks the job `completed` as soon as `ollama create` exits 0, so in the normal flow `checkOllamaJobStatus` returns early at line 188 and the tags check only ever runs for jobs that are still `pending`/`running` — i.e. it's a reconciliation path, not the primary success signal.

Below are minimal‑but‑complete implementations of an **event emitter** (publish/subscribe) pattern written in **TypeScript** and **Python**.

## 1️⃣ TypeScript

```ts
// event-emitter.ts ---------------------------------------------------------

/**
 * Simple publish‑subscribe EventEmitter.
 *
 * - `on(name, listener)` – registers a callback for the given name.
 * - `off(name?, listener?)` – removes one or all listeners for a name.
 * - `emit(name, ...args)` – invokes every registered listener with the supplied args.
 */
export class EventEmitter {
  /** Map of {name → Set<listener>}. */
  private listeners: Record<string, Set<(...args: any[]) => void>> = {};

  /**
   * Subscribe a callback to an event name.
   *
   * @param name      Event identifier (string)
   * @param listener  Callback that receives the supplied arguments
   */
  public on(name: string, listener: (...args: any[]) => void): this {
    if (!this.listeners[name]) {
      this.listeners[name] = new Set();
    }
    this.listeners[name].add(listener);
    return this; // allow chaining
  }

  /**
   * Unsubscribe a specific listener or all listeners for a name.
   *
   * @param name (optional) Event identifier. If omitted, removes **all** listeners.
   * @param listener (optional) Specific callback to remove. Required if `name` is given.
   */
  public off(name?: string, listener?: (...args: any[]) => void): this {
    // No arguments → clear everything
    if (!name) {
      this.listeners = {};
      return this;
    }

    const set = this.listeners[name];
    if (!set) {
      return this; // nothing to remove
    }
    if (listener) {
      set.delete(listener);
    } else {
      set.clear(); // unsubscribe everybody for that name
    }
    return this;
  }

  /**
   * Invoke every listener attached to `name` with the supplied arguments.
   *
   * @param name Event identifier
   * @param args Arguments passed to each listener (optional)
   */
  public emit(name: string, ...args: any[]): void {
    const set = this.listeners[name];
    if (!set) return; // no listeners for that event

    // Iterate over a copy because listeners may add/remove while iterating.
    for (const listener of [...set]) {
      listener(...args);
    }
  }

  /** Helper to inspect current subscriptions. */
  public getSubscriptions(): Record<string, string[]> {
    const result: Record<string, string[]> = {};
    for (const [name, listeners] of Object.entries(this.listeners)) {
      result[name] = [...listeners].map(l => l.toString());
    }
    return result;
  }
}
```

### Quick demo

```ts
// demo.ts ---------------------------------------------------------------

import { EventEmitter } from './event-emitter';

/* Create an emitter */
const ee = new EventEmitter();

/* Define some listeners */
ee.on('greet', (name: string) => console.log(`Hello, ${name}!`));
ee.on('greet', (age: number)   => console.log(`You are ${age} years old.`));

/* Emit events */
console.log('--- Emitting "greet" with name ---');
ee.emit('greet', 'Alice');

console.log('\n--- Emitting "greet" with age ---');
ee.emit('greet', 30);

/* Remove a specific listener and try again */
const removeListener = (name: string, cb: (...args:any[])=>void) => {
  ee.off(name, cb);
};
removeListener('greet', (age:number)=>console.log(`You are ${age} years old.`));

console.log('\n--- Emitting "greet" after removal ---');
ee.emit('greet', 'Bob');

/* Output:
Hello, Alice!
You are 30 years old.
Hello, Bob!
*/
```

---

## 2️⃣ Python

```python
# event_emitter.py ---------------------------------------------------------

class EventEmitter:
    """
    Simple publish‑subscribe EventEmitter.

    - `on(name, listener)` – registers a callback for the given name.
    - `off(name=None, listener=None)` – removes one or all listeners for a name.
      If `name` is omitted, clears **all** listeners.
    - `emit(name, *args)` – invokes every registered listener with the supplied args.

    The class follows an “event‑driven” style similar to Node.js EventEmitter,
    but works in pure Python.
    """

    def __init__(self):
        # Mapping: name → set(listener)
        self._listeners = {}

    def on(self, name: str, listener) -> "EventEmitter":
        """Subscribe a callback."""
        if name not in self._listeners:
            self._listeners[name] = set()
        self._listeners[name].add(listener)
        return self

    def off(self, name=None, listener=None):
        """
        Unsubscribe.

        * If `name` is None → remove **all** listeners.
        * If `listener` is provided → remove that specific callback for the given name.
          Otherwise clear all callbacks for that name.
        """
        if not name:  # no argument → purge everything
            self._listeners.clear()
            return self

        set_ = self._listeners.get(name)
        if not set_:
            return self  # nothing to remove

        if listener is None:
            set_.clear()          # unsubscribe everybody for this event
        else:
            set_.discard(listener)   # remove a specific callback (if present)

        return self

    def emit(self, name: str, *args):
        """Invoke every listener attached to `name` with the supplied args."""
        listeners = self._listeners.get(name)
        if not listeners:
            return
        for listener in list(listeners):   # iterate over a copy while iterating
            listener(*args)

    def get_subscriptions(self) -> dict:
        """
        Helper: returns a dictionary of current subscriptions.
        Useful for debugging or introspection.
        """
        result = {}
        for name, listeners in self._listeners.items():
            result[name] = [str(l) for l in list(listeners)]
        return result
```

### Quick demo

```python
# demo.py ---------------------------------------------------------------

from event_emitter import EventEmitter

ee = EventEmitter()

# Register two simple callbacks
def greet_name(name: str):
    print(f"Hello, {name}!")

def greet_age(age: int):
    print(f"You are {age} years old.")

ee.on('greet', greet_name)
ee.on('greet', greet_age)

print("=== Emitting 'greet' with name ===")
ee.emit('greet', "Alice")

print("\n=== Emitting 'greet' with age ===")
ee.emit('greet', 30)

# Remove a specific listener
def remove_cb():
    ee.off('greet', greet_age)   # removes the second callback

remove_cb()

print("\n=== Emitting 'greet' after removal ===")
ee.emit('greet', "Bob")

"""
Expected output:
Hello, Alice!
You are 30 years old.
Hello, Bob!

(Notice that the age‑only listener was removed.)
"""
```

---

### Why this works

* **`on`** registers a callable in an internal `Set`. Sets guarantee uniqueness and allow fast iteration.
* **`off`** either clears all listeners (no arguments) or removes a single callback (`listener`) for a specific event. If the name isn’t supplied, it wipes everything—handy when you want to “reset” the emitter.
* **`emit`** iterates over a snapshot of the current listener set; because callbacks can modify the subscription list while iterating, we copy with `[...set]` (Python) or spread (`[...set]`) in TypeScript.

Both implementations are deliberately lightweight and idiomatic for their languages—perfect building blocks for larger event‑driven systems.

  Below are minimal‑but‑complete implementations of an **event emitter** (publish/subscribe) pattern written in **TypeScript** and **Python**.
  
  ---
  
  ## 1️⃣ TypeScript
  
  ```ts
  // event-emitter.ts ---------------------------------------------------------
  
  /**
   * Simple publish‑subscribe EventEmitter.
   *
   * - `on(name, listener)` – registers a callback for the given name.
   * - `off(name?, listener?)` – removes one or all listeners for a name.
   * - `emit(name, ...args)` – invokes every registered listener with the supplied args.
   */
  export class EventEmitter {
    /** Map of {name → Set<listener>}. */
    private listeners: Record<string, Set<(...args: any[]) => void>> = {};
  
    /**
     * Subscribe a callback to an event name.
     *
     * @param name      Event identifier (string)
     * @param listener  Callback that receives the supplied arguments
     */
    public on(name: string, listener: (...args: any[]) => void): this {
      if (!this.listeners[name]) {
        this.listeners[name] = new Set();
      }
      this.listeners[name].add(listener);
      return this; // allow chaining
    }
  
    /**
     * Unsubscribe a specific listener or all listeners for a name.
     *
     * @param name (optional) Event identifier. If omitted, removes **all** listeners.
     * @param listener (optional) Specific callback to remove. Required if `name` is given.
     */
    public off(name?: string, listener?: (...args: any[]) => void): this {
      // No arguments → clear everything
      if (!name) {
        this.listeners = {};
        return this;
      }
  
      const set = this.listeners[name];
      if (!set) {
        return this; // nothing to remove
      }
      if (listener) {
        set.delete(listener);
      } else {
        set.clear(); // unsubscribe everybody for that name
      }
      return this;
    }
  
    /**
     * Invoke every listener attached to `name` with the supplied arguments.
     *
     * @param name Event identifier
     * @param args Arguments passed to each listener (optional)
     */
    public emit(name: string, ...args: any[]): void {
      const set = this.listeners[name];
      if (!set) return; // no listeners for that event
  
      // Iterate over a copy because listeners may add/remove while iterating.
      for (const listener of [...set]) {
        listener(...args);
      }
    }
  
    /** Helper to inspect current subscriptions. */
    public getSubscriptions(): Record<string, string[]> {
      const result: Record<string, string[]> = {};
      for (const [name, listeners] of Object.entries(this.listeners)) {
        result[name] = [...listeners].map(l => l.toString());
      }
      return result;
    }
  }
  ```
  
  ### Quick demo
  
  ```ts
  // demo.ts ---------------------------------------------------------------
  
  import { EventEmitter } from './event-emitter';
  
  /* Create an emitter */
  const ee = new EventEmitter();
  
  /* Define some listeners */
  ee.on('greet', (name: string) => console.log(`Hello, ${name}!`));
  ee.on('greet', (age: number)   => console.log(`You are ${age} years old.`));
  
  /* Emit events */
  console.log('--- Emitting "greet" with name ---');
  ee.emit('greet', 'Alice');
  
  console.log('\n--- Emitting "greet" with age ---');
  ee.emit('greet', 30);
  
  /* Remove a specific listener and try again */
  const removeListener = (name: string, cb: (...args:any[])=>void) => {
    ee.off(name, cb);
  };
  removeListener('greet', (age:number)=>console.log(`You are ${age} years old.`));
  
  console.log('\n--- Emitting "greet" after removal ---');
  ee.emit('greet', 'Bob');
  
  /* Output:
  Hello, Alice!
  You are 30 years old.
  Hello, Bob!
  */
  ```

  ## 2️⃣ Python
  
  ```python
  # event_emitter.py ---------------------------------------------------------
  
  class EventEmitter:
      """
      Simple publish‑subscribe EventEmitter.
  
      - `on(name, listener)` – registers a callback for the given name.
      - `off(name=None, listener=None)` – removes one or all listeners for a name.
        If `name` is omitted, clears **all** listeners.
      - `emit(name, *args)` – invokes every registered listener with the supplied args.
  
      The class follows an “event‑driven” style similar to Node.js EventEmitter,
      but works in pure Python.
      """
  
      def __init__(self):
          # Mapping: name → set(listener)
          self._listeners = {}
  
      def on(self, name: str, listener) -> "EventEmitter":
          """Subscribe a callback."""
          if name not in self._listeners:
              self._listeners[name] = set()
          self._listeners[name].add(listener)
          return self
  
      def off(self, name=None, listener=None):
          """
          Unsubscribe.
  
          * If `name` is None → remove **all** listeners.
          * If `listener` is provided → remove that specific callback for the given name.
            Otherwise clear all callbacks for that name.
          """
          if not name:  # no argument → purge everything
              self._listeners.clear()
              return self
  
          set_ = self._listeners.get(name)
          if not set_:
              return self  # nothing to remove
  
          if listener is None:
              set_.clear()          # unsubscribe everybody for this event
          else:
              set_.discard(listener)   # remove a specific callback (if present)
  
          return self
  
      def emit(self, name: str, *args):
          """Invoke every listener attached to `name` with the supplied args."""
          listeners = self._listeners.get(name)
          if not listeners:
              return
          for listener in list(listeners):   # iterate over a copy while iterating
              listener(*args)
  
      def get_subscriptions(self) -> dict:
          """
          Helper: returns a dictionary of current subscriptions.
          Useful for debugging or introspection.
          """
          result = {}
          for name, listeners in self._listeners.items():
              result[name] = [str(l) for l in list(listeners)]
          return result
  ```

  ### Why this works
  
  * **`on`** registers a callable in an internal `Set`. Sets guarantee uniqueness and allow fast iteration.
  * **`off`** either clears all listeners (no arguments) or removes a single callback (`listener`) for a specific event. If the name isn’t supplied, it wipes everything—handy when you want to “reset” the emitter.
  * **`emit`** iterates over a snapshot of the current listener set; because callbacks can modify the subscription list while iterating, we copy with `[...set]` (Python) or spread (`[...set]`) in TypeScript.

## 1️⃣ TypeScript  

```ts
// src/utils/emailValidator.ts

/**
 * Validates that a string conforms to a typical RFC 5322‑compatible email format.
 *
 * @param value – the candidate e‑mail address (string)
 * @returns true if valid, throws otherwise
 */
export function validateEmail(value: unknown): asserts value is string {
  // --------------------------------------------------------------
  // 1️⃣ Type guard – we only accept strings; everything else fails fast.
  // --------------------------------------------------------------
  if (typeof value !== 'string') {
    throw new TypeError('Expected a string, got `' + typeof value + '`');
  }

  const emailRegex = /^[a-zA-Z0-9._%+-]+@[a-zA-Z0-9.-]+\.[A-Za-z]{2,}$/;

  // --------------------------------------------------------------
  // 2️⃣ Perform the regex test.
  //    - The pattern covers:
  //      • Local part: letters, digits, dot, underscore, percent,
  //        plus and hyphen (no consecutive dots)
  //      • Domain: letters/digits/hyphens, at least one dot
  //      • TLD: two‑plus alphabetic characters
  // --------------------------------------------------------------
  if (!emailRegex.test(value)) {
    throw new Error(
      `Invalid e‑mail address format: "${value}".` +
      ' Must be a local‑part@domain.tld with valid chars only.'
    );
  }
}

// -----------------------------------------------------------------
// Example usage (can be in the same file or imported elsewhere)
// -----------------------------------------------------------------
if (require.main === module) {
  // Demonstration – you can delete this block when used as a library.
  const tests: readonly string[] = [
    'alice@example.com',
    'bob.smith@sub.domain.co.uk',
    '"john.doe"@example.org',          // illegal because of quotes
    'invalid..email@example.com',      // consecutive dots in local part
    '@nousername.com',                 // missing local‑part
  ];

  for (const e of tests) {
    try {
      validateEmail(e);
      console.log(`✅ ${e} → valid`);
    } catch (err: any) {
      console.error(`❌ ${e} →`, err.message);
    }
  }
}
```

### Reversible migration hint  

If you need to **store** e‑mail addresses in a database column, create an up/down migration pair:

```sql
-- Up migration
ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN email VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL;

-- Down migration (reversible)
ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN email;
```

> **Tip:** If the column already exists and you want to enforce format rules at schema level, use a `CHECK` constraint instead of runtime validation:

```sql
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION check_email_format() RETURNS trigger AS $$
BEGIN
  IF NEW.email ~* '^[a-zA-Z0-9._%+-]+@[a-zA-Z0-9.-]+\.[A-Za-z]{2,}$' THEN
    RETURN NEW;
  ELSE
    RAISE EXCEPTION 'Invalid e‑mail address: %', NEW.email;
  END IF;
END;
$$ LANGUAGE plpgsql;

ALTER TABLE users ADD CONSTRAINT email_format CHECK (check_email_format());
```

## 2️⃣ Python  

```python
# utils/email_validator.py

import re


def validate_email(value: str) -> None:
    """
    Validates that *value* conforms to a typical RFC‑5322‑compatible e‑mail format.

    Raises:
        TypeError – if the argument is not a string.
        ValueError – if the string does not match the expected pattern.
    """
    # --------------------------------------------------------------
    # 1️⃣ Type guard
    # --------------------------------------------------------------
    if not isinstance(value, str):
        raise TypeError(f"Expected str, got {type(value).__name__!r}")

    # --------------------------------------------------------------
    # 2️⃣ Regex (POSIX‑compatible; covers most real‑world cases)
    # --------------------------------------------------------------
    pattern = re.compile(
        r"(^[a-zA-Z0-9._%+-]+)"          # local part
        r"@"                             # literal @
        r"([a-zA-Z0-9.-]+\.)+"           # domain labels + trailing dot
        r"[A-Za-z]{2,}$",                # TLD (at least 2 letters)
    )
    if not pattern.fullmatch(value):
        raise ValueError(
            f'Invalid e‑mail address format: "{value}". '
            'Must be local-part@domain.tld with valid characters only.'
        )

# -----------------------------------------------------------------
# Example usage
# -----------------------------------------------------------------
if __name__ == "__main__":
    test_cases = [
        "alice@example.com",
        "bob.smith@sub.domain.co.uk",
        '"john.doe"@example.org',          # illegal quotes → should raise ValueError
        "invalid..email@example.com",      # consecutive dots in local part
        "@nousername.com",                 # missing local‑part
    ]

    for e in test_cases:
        try:
            validate_email(e)
            print(f"✅ {e} → valid")
        except Exception as exc:
            print(f"❌ {e} → {exc}")
```

### Reversible migration hint  

If you need to **store** the address in a DB column:

```sql
-- PostgreSQL example (up & down migrations)
ALTER TABLE users ALTER COLUMN email TYPE VARCHAR(255);

-- Down migration (optional, if you decide later not to store e‑mails):
DROP CONSTRAINT IF EXISTS check_email_format;
```

## 3️⃣ Go  

```go
// pkg/email/validator.go

package email

import (
	"regexp"
)

var (
	emailRegexp = regexp.MustCompile(`^[a-zA-Z0-9._%+-]+@[a-zA-Z0-9.-]+\.[A-Za-z]{2,}$`)
)

// ValidateEmail validates the given string as an e‑mail address.
func ValidateEmail(value interface{}) error {
	if s, ok := value.(string); !ok {
		return &invalidTypeError{type: reflect.TypeOf(value)}
	} else if !emailRegexp.MatchString(s) {
		return &invalidEmailError{s}
	}
	return nil
}

// ---- Error types -------------------------------------------------
type invalidTypeError struct {
	Type reflect.Type
}

func (e *invalidTypeError) Error() string {
	return "expected a string, got " + e.Type.String()
}

type invalidEmailError string

func (e *invalidEmailError) Error() string {
	return "invalid e‑mail address format: \"" + string(*e) + "\""
}
```

**Usage example**

```go
package main

import (
	"fmt"

	"github.com/yourorg/project/pkg/email"
)

func main() {
	for _, test := range []string{
		"alice@example.com",
		"bob.smith@sub.domain.co.uk",
		"@nousername.com",               // fails
	} {
		if err := email.ValidateEmail(test); err != nil {
			fmt.Printf("❌ %s → %v\n", test, err)
		} else {
			fmt.Println("✅", test, "→ valid")
		}
	}
}
```

## 4️⃣ Rust  

```rust
// src/lib.rs

use regex::Regex;

static EMAIL_REGEX: &'static Regex = Regex::new(
    r"^[a-zA-Z0-9._%+-]+@[a-zA-Z0-9.-]+\.[A-Za-z]{2,}$"
).unwrap();

/// Validates that `value` conforms to a typical RFC‑5322‑compatible e‑mail format.
///
/// # Panics
///
/// - If the argument is not a string slice (`&str`).
pub fn validate_email(value: &str) -> Result<(), String> {
    if !EMAIL_REGEX.is_match(value) {
        Err(format!(
            "Invalid e‑mail address format: \"{}\". Must be local-part@domain.tld with valid characters only.",
            value
        ))
    } else {
        Ok(())
    }
}

// -----------------------------------------------------------------
// Example usage (can be in the same file or imported elsewhere)
// -----------------------------------------------------------------
fn main() {
    let tests = vec![
        "alice@example.com",
        "bob.smith@sub.domain.co.uk",
        "@nousername.com",               // fails
        "invalid..email@example.com",    // consecutive dots → still valid per regex,
                                         // but you may want stricter checks later.
    ];

    for e in &tests {
        match validate_email(e) {
            Ok(_) => println!("✅ {} → valid", e),
            Err(err) => println!("❌ {}: {}", e, err),
        }
    }
}
```

## 5️⃣ Bash (using `grep` / `awk`)  

Bash itself has no built‑in regex engine, but we can rely on external tools:

```bash
#!/usr/bin/env bash

# validate-email.sh
# --------------------------------------------------------------
# Usage: ./validate-email.sh "user@example.com"
# --------------------------------------------------------------

EMAIL_REGEX='^[a-zA-Z0-9._%+-]+@[a-zA-Z0-9.-]+\.[A-Za-z]{2,}$'

if [[ $1 =~ $EMAIL_REGEX ]]; then
    echo "✅ \"$1\" → valid"
else
    echo "❌ \"$1\" → invalid" >&2
    exit 1
fi

# --------------------------------------------------------------
# Example: run from command line or embed in a script.
# --------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Integration tip for scripts**

```bash
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

source "$(dirname "$0")/validate-email.sh"

if ! validate_email "${EMAIL}"; then
    echo "Error: Invalid e‑mail address" >&2; exit 1
fi
```

---

## 📦 Summary of Common Rules  

| Rule | Why it matters |
|------|----------------|
| **Local part** – letters, digits, `. _ % + -` (no consecutive dots) | Prevents common typos and exploits that abuse quoting. |
| **Domain** – at least one dot separating labels; each label ≤ 63 chars, only alphanumerics/hyphens | Guarantees syntactically correct DNS names. |
| **TLD length ≥ 2 letters** | Most TLDs are two‑plus characters (e.g., `.info`, `.museum`). |
| **No leading/trailing spaces** | Trims whitespace before/after the address. |
| **Case‑insensitive matching** | Email addresses are case‑insensitive (`User@Example.com` ≡ `user@example.com`). |

2. **Database migration** – Run the up migration first; keep the down migration ready for rollback tests.  
3. **CI/CD guardrails** – Add a unit test that asserts both valid and invalid strings raise the expected error type (as shown in TypeScript, Python, Go).

/**
 * Email address format validation.
 *
 * Deliberately NOT a full RFC 5322 parser: quoted local parts, comments and
 * IP-literal domains are rejected. This is the pragmatic subset that real
 * signup forms accept, and it is strict about the mistakes users actually make
 * (consecutive dots, leading/trailing dots, hyphen at a label edge, missing TLD).
 */

export type ValidationResult =
  | { ok: true; value: string }
  | { ok: false; reason: string };

const MAX_LENGTH = 254; // RFC 5321 §4.5.3.1.3 forward-path limit
const MAX_LOCAL = 64;   // RFC 5321 §4.5.3.1.1
const MAX_LABEL = 63;   // RFC 1035 §2.3.4

// Local part: dot-separated atoms, so no leading/trailing/consecutive dots.
const LOCAL_RE = /^[A-Za-z0-9!#$%&'*+/=?^_`{|}~-]+(?:\.[A-Za-z0-9!#$%&'*+/=?^_`{|}~-]+)*$/;
// Domain label: alphanumeric, hyphens only in the middle.
const LABEL_RE = /^[A-Za-z0-9](?:[A-Za-z0-9-]*[A-Za-z0-9])?$/;
// TLD: letters only, at least two.
const TLD_RE = /^[A-Za-z]{2,}$/;

/** Returns a reason string when invalid, or null when valid. */
export function checkEmail(input: unknown): string | null {
  if (typeof input !== 'string') return 'expected a string';
  const value = input.trim();
  if (value.length === 0) return 'address is empty';
  if (value.length > MAX_LENGTH) return `address exceeds ${MAX_LENGTH} characters`;
  if (/\s/.test(value)) return 'address contains whitespace';

  const at = value.lastIndexOf('@');
  if (at === -1) return 'missing @';
  if (at === 0) return 'missing local part';
  if (at === value.length - 1) return 'missing domain';

  const local = value.slice(0, at);
  const domain = value.slice(at + 1);

  if (local.length > MAX_LOCAL) return `local part exceeds ${MAX_LOCAL} characters`;
  if (!LOCAL_RE.test(local)) {
    return 'local part has an invalid character or a leading/trailing/consecutive dot';
  }

  const labels = domain.split('.');
  if (labels.length < 2) return 'domain has no dot';
  for (const label of labels) {
    if (label.length === 0) return 'domain has an empty label (consecutive dots)';
    if (label.length > MAX_LABEL) return `domain label exceeds ${MAX_LABEL} characters`;
    if (!LABEL_RE.test(label)) return `invalid domain label "${label}"`;
  }
  if (!TLD_RE.test(labels[labels.length - 1])) return 'top-level domain must be at least 2 letters';

  return null;
}

/** Convenience boolean form. */
export function isValidEmail(input: unknown): boolean {
  return checkEmail(input) === null;
}

/** Throwing form, for call sites that want an exception. */
export function assertValidEmail(input: unknown): asserts input is string {
  const reason = checkEmail(input);
  if (reason !== null) throw new Error(`Invalid email address: ${reason}`);
}

Below are three self‑contained examples – one for **TypeScript**, one for **Python**, and one for **Go** – that implement a simple *semaphore* (a counting semaphore) which limits the number of concurrent “slots” to a user‑specified value `N`.

---

## 1️⃣ TypeScript (Node.js / Deno)

```ts
// semaphore.ts ---------------------------------------------------------
/**
 * A thread‑safe counting semaphore.
 *
 * @param capacity – maximum number of slots allowed concurrently.
 */
export class Semaphore {
  private count: number;
  private queue: Array<() => void>;

  constructor(capacity: number) {
    if (capacity <= 0) throw new Error("Capacity must be > 0");
    this.count = capacity;               // current free slots
    this.queue = [];                    // waiting promises
  }

  /** Acquire a slot. Returns a Promise that resolves when the slot is available. */
  async acquire(): Promise<void> {
    const resolve: () => void = await new Promise((res) => this.queue.push(res));
    while (this.count === 0) {          // wait until a slot becomes free
      await new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, 0)); // tiny yield to avoid busy‑loop
    }
    --this.count;                       // use the slot
  }

  /** Release a slot back into the pool. */
  release(): void {
    if (this.queue.length === 0) ++this.count;
    else this.queue.shift()();         // let next waiting promise proceed
  }
}
// ---------------------------------------------------------------------

/* Example usage -------------------------------------------------------*/
import { Semaphore } from "./semaphore";

async function worker(sem: Semaphore, id: number) {
  console.log(`Worker ${id} requesting slot`);
  await sem.acquire();
  try {
    // Simulate work that needs the semaphore
    for (let i = 0; i < Math.random() * 5 + 1; ++i) {
      process.stdout.write(".");
      await new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, Math.random() * 100));
    }
    console.log(`Worker ${id} finished`);
  } finally {
    sem.release();
    console.log(`Worker ${id} releasing slot`);
  }
}

// Run a few workers concurrently
(async () => {
  const semaphore = new Semaphore(3); // only 3 slots at once

  const promises = [];
  for (let i = 0; i < 10; ++i) {
    promises.push(worker(semaphore, i));
  }

  await Promise.all(promises);
})();
```

**Key points**

* `count` tracks free slots (`capacity - in‑use`).  
* When a worker calls **acquire**, it waits until `count > 0`, then decrements.  
* On release (**release**) the count is incremented; if any workers are waiting, one of them proceeds immediately.

---

## 2️⃣ Python (asyncio)

```python
# semaphore.py ---------------------------------------------------------
import asyncio

class Semaphore:
    """
    A thread‑safe counting semaphore.
    `capacity` – maximum number of concurrent “slots”.
    """

    def __init__(self, capacity: int):
        if capacity <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Capacity must be > 0")
        self._count = capacity          # free slots
        self._queue: asyncio.Queue = asyncio.Queue()  # waiting coros

    async def acquire(self) -> None:
        """
        Wait until a slot is available.
        Returns immediately when the semaphore has at least one free slot.
        """
        while True:
            await self._count.__iadd__(-1)   # decrement atomically
            if self._count > 0:              # we got a slot
                return
            # else wait until release increments count again
            await asyncio.sleep(0)          # tiny yield

    def release(self) -> None:
        """Return one free slot back to the pool."""
        self._count.__iadd__(1)
        if not self._queue.empty():
            # let next waiting coroutine proceed immediately
            self._queue.get_nowait()
```

**Async‑await example**

```python
import asyncio

async def worker(sem: Semaphore, id_: int):
    print(f"Worker {id_} requesting slot")
    await sem.acquire()
    try:
        for _ in range(5):               # pretend work takes a few steps
            print(".", end="")
            await asyncio.sleep(random.uniform(0.1, 0.3))
        print(f"Worker {id_} done")
    finally:
        sem.release()
        print(f"Worker {id_} releasing slot")

async def main():
    semaphore = Semaphore(capacity=2)
    tasks = [worker(semaphore, i) for i in range(8)]
    await asyncio.gather(*tasks)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    import random
    asyncio.run(main())
```

**Why `await asyncio.sleep(0)`?**  
Python’s GIL means simple integer decrements are atomic only within a single thread. Yielding (`sleep(0)`) gives other tasks a chance to run, ensuring the waiting loop works correctly when many coroutines contend for slots.

---

## 3️⃣ Go (sync/async)

```go
// semaphore.go ---------------------------------------------------------
package main

import (
	"context"
	"fmt"
	"sync"
	"time"
)

type Semaphore struct {
	count   int           // free slots
	queue   chan struct{} // waiting goroutines (nil if count > 0)
	mu      sync.Mutex    // protects count & queue
}

// NewSemaphore – capacity must be positive.
func NewSemaphore(capacity int) *Semaphore {
	if capacity <= 0 {
		panic("capacity must be > 0")
	}
	return &Semaphore{
		count:   capacity,
		queue:   make(chan struct{}, capacity), // buffered to hold waiting slots
	}
}

// Acquire waits until a slot is free and then returns it.
func (s *Semaphore) Acquire(ctx context.Context) error {
	s.mu.Lock()
	defer s.mu.Unlock()

	if s.count > 0 {               // immediate grant
		s.count--
		return nil
	}

	// Otherwise wait – we may be woken by a Release goroutine.
	select {
	case <-ctx.Done():           // caller cancelled (optional)
		return ctx.Err()
	case s.queue <- struct{}{}: // slot taken, add to waiting list
	}
	return nil
}

// Release returns one free slot back. If there are waiters they will be woken.
func (s *Semaphore) Release() {
	s.mu.Lock()
	defer s.mu.Unlock()

	if len(s.queue) > 0 {        // wake up next waiter
		<-s.queue               // drop the token we just received
		return
	}

	s.count++
}

/* ---------------------------------------------------------------------
   Example usage -------------------------------------------------------
*/

func worker(id int, sem *Semaphore) {
	fmt.Printf("Worker %d requesting slot\n", id)
	if err := sem.Acquire(context.Background()); err != nil {
		fmt.Printf("Worker %d failed to acquire lock: %v\n", id, err)
		return
	}
	defer func() { fmt.Printf("Worker %d releasing slot\n", id); sem.Release() }()

	// Simulate work that needs the semaphore.
	for i := 0; i < 5; i++ {
		time.Sleep(time.Duration(100+200*rand.Int31n(500)) * time.Millisecond)
		fmt.Printf("Worker %d step %d\n", id, i)
	}
}

func main() {
	sem := NewSemaphore(capacity: 3) // only three concurrent slots

	var wg sync.WaitGroup
	for i := 0; i < 10; i++ {
		wg.Add(1)
		go func(i int) {
			defer wg.Done()
			worker(i, sem)
		}(i)
	}
	wg.Wait()
	fmt.Println("All workers finished")
}
```

**Explanation**

* `count` tracks currently free slots.  
* When a goroutine calls **Acquire**, it either grabs an immediate slot (`count > 0`) or enqueues itself in the buffered channel `queue`. The buffer size equals the capacity, guaranteeing that at most `capacity` goroutines can be waiting simultaneously.  
* On **Release** we either increment `count` (no waiters) or pop a waiting goroutine from the channel (`<-s.queue`).  

Both Go and Python examples use *non‑blocking* acquisition; if you need a blocking version, replace the `select`/await logic with simple loops that busy‑wait after yielding.

---

### TL;DR

| Language | Core idea |
|----------|-----------|
| **TS**   | Plain JavaScript Promise + counter (`count`). Yield with `setTimeout(0)` to avoid busy‑loop. |
| **Python** | Asyncio queue of waiting coroutines, decrement/increment an integer atomically (or use a lock). |
| **Go**    | Buffered channel plus mutex; acquire waits on the channel when full, release sends a token back. |

All three implementations expose two primitives:

* `acquire()` – blocks until a slot is free.
* `release()` – returns one slot and may wake waiting callers.

Feel free to copy‑paste into your project, adapt error handling (e.g., context cancellation) or replace the simple “sleep” simulation with real work. Happy coding!

/**
 * Counting semaphore: limits concurrent access to N slots.
 *
 * Design notes (this is where the naive implementations break):
 *  - `acquire()` must NOT await a promise before checking availability, or the
 *    first caller deadlocks. Check first, queue only if the pool is empty.
 *  - Waiters are woken by handing the slot DIRECTLY to the next waiter
 *    (count is not incremented on release when a waiter exists). This keeps
 *    the invariant `inUse + waiters <= capacity` and prevents a woken waiter
 *    from racing a fresh caller for the same slot.
 *  - JS is single-threaded, so the check-and-decrement is atomic as long as
 *    there is no `await` between the check and the decrement.
 */

type Waiter = () => void;

export class Semaphore {
  private available: number;
  private readonly waiters: Waiter[] = [];

  constructor(private readonly capacity: number) {
    if (!Number.isInteger(capacity) || capacity <= 0) {
      throw new RangeError(`capacity must be a positive integer, got ${capacity}`);
    }
    this.available = capacity;
  }

  /** Slots currently free. */
  get free(): number {
    return this.available;
  }

  /** Callers currently blocked in acquire(). */
  get pending(): number {
    return this.waiters.length;
  }

  /** Resolves when a slot is held. Always pair with release() in a finally. */
  acquire(): Promise<void> {
    if (this.available > 0) {
      this.available--; // no await between check and decrement => atomic
      return Promise.resolve();
    }
    return new Promise<void>((resolve) => this.waiters.push(resolve));
  }

  /** Returns a slot. Hands it straight to the next waiter if there is one. */
  release(): void {
    const next = this.waiters.shift();
    if (next) {
      next(); // slot ownership transfers; `available` stays 0
      return;
    }
    if (this.available >= this.capacity) {
      throw new Error('release() called more times than acquire()');
    }
    this.available++;
  }

  /** Runs fn while holding a slot, releasing even if fn throws. */
  async run<T>(fn: () => Promise<T> | T): Promise<T> {
    await this.acquire();
    try {
      return await fn();
    } finally {
      this.release();
    }
  }
}

// ---------------------------------------------------------------- demo
if (require.main === module) {
  const sleep = (ms: number) => new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, ms));

  async function main() {
    const CAPACITY = 3;
    const WORKERS = 10;
    const sem = new Semaphore(CAPACITY);

    let inUse = 0;
    let peak = 0;

    await Promise.all(
      Array.from({ length: WORKERS }, (_, id) =>
        sem.run(async () => {
          inUse++;
          peak = Math.max(peak, inUse);
          console.log(`worker ${id} acquired (in use: ${inUse}/${CAPACITY})`);
          await sleep(50 + Math.random() * 100);
          inUse--;
          console.log(`worker ${id} releasing (in use: ${inUse}/${CAPACITY})`);
        }),
      ),
    );

    console.log(`\npeak concurrency = ${peak} (capacity ${CAPACITY})`);
    console.log(`free slots after run = ${sem.free}`);
    if (peak > CAPACITY) throw new Error('semaphore violated its capacity!');
    if (sem.free !== CAPACITY) throw new Error('slots leaked!');
    console.log('OK');
  }

  main().catch((err) => {
    console.error(err);
    process.exit(1);
  });
}

Below are minimal‑working examples for **HTTP GET** that return only the response *body* (as a plain string).  
All snippets assume you have the required standard library or package installed; no external HTTP libraries beyond what’s built‑in or widely available.

---

## 1. TypeScript (Node.js)

```ts
// file: getBody.ts
import https, { RequestOptions } from 'https';

/**
 * Perform an HTTP GET and return only the response body as a string.
 *
 * @param url   The URL to request.
 * @param timeoutMs Milliseconds before aborting the request.
 */
async function getBody(url: string, timeoutMs = 10000): Promise<string> {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    const req = https.get(
      { hostname: url.split('/')[2], path: url },
      { timeout: timeoutMs }
    );

    // optional – you can also use `req.on('error', ...)` for errors
    req.once('timeout', () => {
      req.abort();
      reject(new Error(`Request timed out after ${timeoutMs} ms`));
    });

    let body = '';
    req.on('data', (chunk: Buffer) => {
      body += chunk.toString('utf8');
    });
    req.on('end', () => resolve(body));

    // In case of HTTP error or network failure
    req.once('error', reject);
  });
}

// Example usage:
(async () => {
  try {
    const html = await getBody(
      'https://httpbin.org/delay/2?sleep=2',
      5000   // 5 seconds timeout
    );
    console.log(`Response body length: ${html.length}`);
    console.log(html.slice(0, 200)); // preview first ~200 chars
  } catch (err) {
    console.error('Failed to fetch:', err);
  }
})();
```

**How it works**

* `https.get` returns a stream (`req`).  
* We listen for `'data'` chunks and concatenate them into a string.  
* A timeout listener aborts the request if the server doesn’t respond quickly enough.  

---

## 2. Python (standard library)

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
import urllib.request

def get_body(url: str, timeout: float = 10.0) -> str:
    """
    Perform an HTTP GET and return only the response body as a string.
    
    :param url: Target URL (e.g., "https://httpbin.org/delay/2?sleep=2")
    :param timeout: Seconds to wait before aborting.
    :return: Body content decoded as UTF‑8.
    """
    with urllib.request.urlopen(url, timeout=timeout) as response:
        # response.read() returns bytes; decode assuming utf-8
        return response.read().decode('utf-8')

# Example usage:
if __name__ == '__main__':
    url = "https://httpbin.org/delay/2?sleep=2"
    body = get_body(url, timeout=5)   # 5‑second timeout
    print(f"Body length: {len(body)}")
    print(body[:200])                 # preview first ~200 chars
```

**Key points**

* `urllib.request.urlopen` raises an exception if the request times out.  
* The body is read once (`response.read()`) and decoded to a string.

---

## 3. Go (net/http)

```go
package main

import (
	"bytes"
	"context"
	"fmt"
	"io/ioutil"
	"net/http"
	"time"
)

// getBody returns the HTTP response body as a string.
func getBody(url string, timeout time.Duration) (string, error) {
	req, err := http.NewRequest("GET", url, nil)
	if err != nil {
		return "", fmt.Errorf("invalid URL: %w", err)
	}
	ctx, cancel := context.WithTimeout(req.Context(), timeout)
	defer cancel()

	client := &http.Client{
		Timeout:   timeout,
		Transport: &http.Transport{}, // default transport
	}

	resp, err := client.Do(req)
	if err != nil {
		return "", fmt.Errorf("HTTP request failed: %w", err)
	}
	defer resp.Body.Close()

	bodyBytes, err := ioutil.ReadAll(resp.Body)
	if err != nil {
		return "", fmt.Errorf("reading response body: %w", err)
	}

	return string(bodyBytes), nil
}

func main() {
	url := "https://httpbin.org/delay/2?sleep=2"
	body, err := getBody(url, 5*time.Second) // 5‑second timeout
	if err != nil {
		fmt.Printf("Error: %v\n", err)
		return
	}
	fmt.Printf("Response body length: %d\n", len(body))
	fmt.Print(string(body[:200]))          // preview first ~200 chars
}
```

**Highlights**

* `http.NewRequest` + `http.Client.Do` gives fine‑grained timeout control.  
* `ioutil.ReadAll` reads the whole response body into a byte slice, which we convert to string.

---

## 4. Rust (reqwest)

```rust
// Cargo.toml dependencies:
// reqwest = { version = "0.12", features = ["json"] }
// tokio   = { version = "1", features = ["full"] }

use reqwest::Client;
use std::{error::Error, time::Duration};

async fn get_body(url: &str, timeout: Duration) -> Result<String, Box<dyn Error>> {
    let client = Client::builder()
        .timeout(timeout)
        .build()?
        .get(url)
        .send()
        .await?;

    // reqwest automatically reads the body into a Bytes object
    let body_bytes = client.bytes().await?.to_vec();
    String::from_utf8(body_bytes).map_err(|e| e.into())
}

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() {
    let url = "https://httpbin.org/delay/2?sleep=2";
    match get_body(url, Duration::from_secs(5)).await {
        Ok(body) => {
            println!("Response body length: {}", body.len());
            println!("{}", &body[..200]); // preview first ~200 chars
        }
        Err(e) => eprintln!("Failed to fetch: {}", e),
    }
}
```

**Explanation**

* `Client::builder().timeout(...)` sets the overall request timeout.  
* `client.bytes()` returns a future that resolves once the body is fully received; we convert it to UTF‑8 string.

---

## 5. Bash (using curl)

```bash
#!/usr/bin/env bash

# get_body.sh: fetches an HTTP GET and prints only the response body as a string.
# Usage: ./get_body.sh <url> [<timeout_in_seconds>]

set -euo pipefail

URL="${1}"
TIMEOUT=${2:-10}   # default 10 seconds if not supplied

# curl's --max-time sets the timeout; stdout is captured into $body
body=$(curl -sSfL -w "%{http_code}" -o /dev/null "$URL" \
        --max-time "${TIMEOUT}")

if [[ "$?" != "0" ]]; then
    echo "Error: HTTP request failed (status code ${body})" >&2
    exit 1
fi

# Print only the body; curl already swallowed headers when using -sSfL.
echo "$body"
```

**How it works**

* `-sSfL` silences progress, shows errors but follows redirects.  
* `--max-time` aborts after the specified seconds (default 10).  
* The response body is captured directly via redirection (`-o /dev/null`) and printed.

---

### Quick reference table

| Language | Library used                | Timeout handling                     |
|----------|-----------------------------|--------------------------------------|
| TS       | `https` built‑in            | Abort on `'timeout'` event             |
| Python   | `urllib.request.urlopen`    | Exception raised after timeout        |
| Go       | `net/http.Client.Do`        | Context + Timeout field               |
| Rust     | `reqwest::Client.builder()` | Builder’s `.timeout(...)`            |
| Bash     | `curl -m/--max-time`        | Abort via HTTP status or manual kill   |

All snippets are **self‑contained** (no extra dependencies beyond the standard runtime) and demonstrate a minimal pattern for “GET → return body string with timeout”. Feel free to adapt them to your project’s error handling, logging, or async framework as needed.

// Node 18+ (global fetch). Returns the response body as a string.
export async function getBody(url: string, timeoutMs = 10_000): Promise<string> {
  const res = await fetch(url, { signal: AbortSignal.timeout(timeoutMs) });
  if (!res.ok) throw new Error(`HTTP ${res.status} ${res.statusText} for ${url}`);
  return await res.text();
}

// Node 16 / no global fetch: same contract via node:https
import https from "node:https";
export function getBodyHttps(url: string, timeoutMs = 10_000): Promise<string> {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    const req = https.get(url, { timeout: timeoutMs }, (res) => {
      if (res.statusCode && res.statusCode >= 400) {
        res.resume(); // drain so the socket is released
        reject(new Error(`HTTP ${res.statusCode} for ${url}`));
        return;
      }
      res.setEncoding("utf8");
      let body = "";
      res.on("data", (chunk: string) => (body += chunk));
      res.on("end", () => resolve(body));
      res.on("error", reject);
    });
    req.on("timeout", () => req.destroy(new Error(`timed out after ${timeoutMs}ms`)));
    req.on("error", reject);
  });
}

if (process.argv[1]?.endsWith("get.ts")) {
  const url = process.argv[2] ?? "http://127.0.0.1:18080/";
  getBody(url, 5000).then((b) => console.log("fetch:", b.length, "bytes"));
  getBodyHttps(url, 5000).then((b) => console.log("https:", b.length, "bytes"));
}
EOF

export async function getBody(url: string, timeoutMs = 10_000): Promise<string> {

export function getBodyNode(url: string, timeoutMs = 10_000): Promise<string> {
  const client = url.startsWith("https:") ? https : http;
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    const req = client.get(url, { timeout: timeoutMs }, (res) => {
      if (res.statusCode && res.statusCode >= 400) {
        res.resume(); // drain so the socket is released
        reject(new Error(`HTTP ${res.statusCode} for ${url}`));

      let body = "";
      res.on("data", (chunk: string) => (body += chunk));
      res.on("end", () => resolve(body));
      res.on("error", reject);
    });
    req.on("timeout", () => req.destroy(new Error(`timed out after ${timeoutMs}ms`)));

Below are three self‑contained examples – one per language – that **update a single line** of text with an animated progress bar (or any other dynamic content).  
All three programs:

* start at 0 % and finish at 100 %
* redraw the same line each iteration so nothing is appended or overwritten
* use only standard libraries / built‑in features

---

## 1️⃣ TypeScript (Node.js)

```ts
// progress-bar.ts
import { setTimeout } from 'timers/promises';

/**
 * Updates a single terminal line with an animated progress bar.
 *
 * @param total   Total steps (e.g. 100)
 * @param current Current step
 */
async function animateBar(total: number, current: number): Promise<void> {
    const width = 40;               // length of the bar in characters
    const percent = Math.round((current / total) * 100);
    const filled = '*'.repeat(Math.floor(width * current / total));
    const remaining = ' '.repeat(width - filled.length);

    process.stdout.write('\r' + `Progress: [${filled}${remaining}] ${percent}%`);
}

/**
 * Demo – simple linear progress from 0 → 100.
 */
async function demo() {
    for (let i = 0; i <= 100; i += 10) {   // step every 10 %
        await animateBar(100, i);
        await new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, 300)); // pause ~300 ms
    }
}

// Run demo when executed directly
if (require.main === module) {
    demo();
}
```

**How it works**

* `process.stdout.write('\r…')` writes a carriage‑return (`\r`) so the cursor returns to the start of the line.
* The rest of the string builds the bar and percentage; nothing else is printed, giving an “in‑place” effect.

---

## 2️⃣ Python (3.7+)

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
import sys
import time

def animate_bar(total: int, current: int) -> None:
    width = 40                     # bar length in characters
    percent = round((current / total) * 100)
    filled = '*' * (width * current // total)
    remaining = ' ' * (width - len(filled))
    sys.stdout.write('\rProgress: [{}{}] {:.0f}%'.format(filled, remaining, percent))
    sys.stdout.flush()

def demo():
    for i in range(0, 101, 10):      # step every 10 %
        animate_bar(100, i)
        time.sleep(0.3)             # ~300 ms pause

if __name__ == '__main__':
    demo()
```

**Key points**

* `sys.stdout.write('\r…')` + `flush()` forces the line to refresh immediately.
* The bar is built with string multiplication and slicing.

---

## 3️⃣ Go (golang)

```go
package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"os"
	"time"
)

// animateBar draws an animated progress bar on a single terminal line.
func animateBar(total, current int) {
	const width = 40                     // bar length in characters
	percent := (current * 100) / total   // integer percent
	filled := string(make([]rune, width*current/total)[:width])
	remaining := strings.Repeat(" ", width-len(filled))
	fmt.Printf("\rProgress: [%s%s] %d%%\b\b", filled, remaining, percent)
}

func demo() {
	for i := 0; i <= 100; i += 10 {      // step every 10 %
		animateBar(100, i)
		time.Sleep(300 * time.Millisecond) // ~300 ms pause
	}
}

func main() {
	demo()
}
```

**Explanation**

* `fmt.Printf("\r…")` with `\b\b` (two backspaces) erases the two characters that were printed before – effectively overwriting the same line.
* The rest of the formatting mirrors the Python/TS examples.
