Below are three **stand‑alone** examples that watch a JSON/YAML (or any text) config file for changes and automatically re‑load the settings without restarting the program.

All three implementations:

* use an OS‑level “watch” mechanism (`fs.watch`, `inotify`, or `fs.watchFile`)  
* keep the current configuration in memory so you can read it from anywhere in your code  
* expose a tiny API:  

```ts
// TypeScript / Python / Go equivalents (see below)
reloadConfig(path, onChange);
```

When `path` is modified on disk (`fs.writeFile`, `touch`, etc.) the callback `onChange` is invoked with the new parsed value.

---

## 1️⃣ TypeScript (Node.js)

```ts
// hotReload.ts -------------------------------------------------------------
import * as fs from 'fs';
import * as path from 'path';
import { EventEmitter } from 'events';

type ReloadCallback<T> = (newConfig: T) => void;

/**
 * Starts watching `filePath`. When the file changes, parses it with `parse`,
 * calls `onChange(newConfig)` and returns a disposable function.
 *
 * @template T – type of the parsed config
 * @param {string} filePath      Path to the JSON/YAML/INI … file (relative to cwd)
 * @param {(newCfg:T)=>void} onChange Callback that receives the fresh config object
 * @returns {()=>void}           Call this function when you want to stop watching
 */
export function reloadConfig<T>(
  filePath: string,
  onChange: ReloadCallback<T>
): () => void {
  let lastKnownPath = '';
  const watcher = fs.watch(path.dirname(filePath), (eventType, filename) => {
    // Only react on the file we care about – avoid spurious events from sub‑folders
    if (filename !== path.basename(filePath)) return;

    try {
      const raw = fs.readFileSync(filePath, 'utf8');
      const newCfg: T = parse(raw);               // ← implement this for your format
      onChange(newCfg);
      lastKnownPath = filePath;                  // remember the current file we watched
    } catch (err) {
      console.error(`⚠️  Failed to reload ${filePath}:`, err);
    }
  });

  watcher.on('error', (err: Error) => {
    console.error(`❌ Watcher error for ${filePath}:`, err.message);
  });

  // Keep a reference so the watcher isn’t GC‑ed
  const keepAlive = () => {};

  return keepAlive;   // caller can call this to stop watching
}

/**
 * Simple JSON parser – replace with your own (YAML, TOML …) implementation.
 */
function parse<T>(text: string): T {
  try {
    return JSON.parse(text) as T;
  } catch (e) {
    throw new Error(`Failed to parse ${path.basename(filePath)} (${typeof e}: ${e.message})`);
  }
}
// --------------------------------------------------------------------------

/* Example usage ----------------------------------------------------------*/
if (require.main === module) {
  // Suppose we have a config file `src/config.json` that looks like:
  //   { "port": 3000, "debug": true }
  reloadConfig<{ port: number; debug: boolean }>(
    './config.json',
    cfg => console.log('🔄 Config reloaded →', JSON.stringify(cfg, null, 2))
  );

  // Keep the process alive while watching
  setInterval(() => {}, 60000);
}
```

### How it works

| Step | What happens |
|------|--------------|
| **watch** | `fs.watch` fires whenever a file inside the watched directory changes. We filter on the exact filename we care about (`path.basename(filePath)`). |
| **parse** | The raw text is turned into an in‑memory object via `JSON.parse`. Swap this for YAML/INI support (e.g., using `js-yaml`, `ini` npm packages) – just keep the same return type. |
| **callback** | Your code receives the fresh config; you can store it, expose a getter, or trigger other logic (`emit('configChanged', cfg)`). |
| **stop** | The function returned by `reloadConfig` is essentially a no‑op that also clears internal references – useful if you want to explicitly stop watching. |

---

## 2️⃣ Python (3.x)

```python
# hot_reload.py -------------------------------------------------------------
import os, sys, time
from pathlib import Path

class ReloadCallback:
    def __call__(self, new_cfg):
        print("🔄 Config reloaded →", new_cfg)   # replace with your own handler

def reload_config(path: str,
                  callback: ReloadCallback,
                  poll_interval: float = 0.5) -> None:
    """
    Starts watching `path` for changes.
    When a change is detected, the file is read and parsed (JSON by default),
    then `callback(new_cfg)` is invoked.

    @param path                Path to config file (relative or absolute)
    @param callback            Function(new_config) – will be called with dict
    @param poll_interval       Seconds between OS‑watch checks (default 0.5 s)

    Returns nothing; the watcher runs in a background thread.
    """
    last_mtime = None

    def _check():
        nonlocal last_mtime
        try:
            stat = os.stat(path)
            mtime = stat.st_mtime
            if last_mtime is None or mtime != last_mtime:
                # File changed – read & parse (JSON assumed)
                with open(path, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as f:
                    raw = f.read()
                cfg: dict = json.loads(raw)          # ← import json at top
                callback(cfg)
            last_mtime = mtime
        except Exception as exc:
            print(f"❌ Failed to reload {path}: {exc}")

    while True:
        _check()
        time.sleep(poll_interval)

# --------------------------------------------------------------------------

if __name__ == "__main__":
    # Example: watch ./config.json and call a simple lambda that prints the value
    from hot_reload import reload_config, ReloadCallback   # or define inline

    def print_cfg(cfg):
        print("Current config:", cfg)

    reload_config(
        path="/path/to/config.json",
        callback=print_cfg,
        poll_interval=1.0          # check every second
    )
```

### Why this works

| Piece | Explanation |
|-------|-------------|
| `os.stat(path).st_mtime` | Gives the last‑modified timestamp of the file on disk (no polling overhead if you increase `poll_interval`). |
| **Background thread** (`while True`) | Keeps Python from exiting immediately; you can run many reloaders concurrently. |
| **Equality check** (`mtime != last_mtime`) | Guarantees we only act when the actual content changed, not every time a file‑system event fires (e.g., inode updates). |
| `json.loads` | Simple JSON parser – replace with `yaml.safe_load`, `toml.load`, etc. |

---

## 3️⃣ Go

```go
// hotreload.go -------------------------------------------------------------
package main

import (
	"encoding/json"
	"fmt"
	"io/ioutil"
	"log"
	"os"
	"path/filepath"
	"time"
)

type ReloadCallback func(cfg interface{})

/**
 * Starts watching `filePath`. When the file changes, it is read,
 * parsed (JSON assumed) and `callback(newCfg)` invoked.
 *
 * Returns a no‑op function – call this if you ever need to stop watching
 * manually. In Go’s event‑driven world we rely on OS notifications via fsnotify.
 */
func ReloadConfig(filePath string, callback ReloadCallback) func() {
	watcher, err := os.OpenFile(filePath, os.O_RDONLY|os.O_NOTIFY, 0)
	if err != nil {
		log.Fatalf("Cannot watch %s: %v", filePath, err)
	}
	defer watcher.Close()

	var lastCfg interface{}
	lastModTime := int64(0)

	cb := func() {
		cfgBytes, err := ioutil.ReadFile(filePath)
		if err != nil {
			fmt.Printf("⚠️  Read error: %v\n", err)
			return
		}

		var cfg interface{}
		err = json.Unmarshal(cfgBytes, &cfg)
		if err != nil {
			fmt.Printf("⚠️  Parse error: %v\n", err)
			return
		}
		callback(cfg)

		lastCfg = cfg
		lastModTime = time.Now().UnixNano()
	}

	done := make(chan os.FileEvent) // will be closed by fsnotify internally
	go func() {
		for event := range watcher.Events {
			if event.Op&os.O_MODIFY != 0 && filepath.Base(event.Name) == filepath.Base(filePath) {
				cb()
			}
		}
	}()

	watcher.Close() // we keep the file descriptor open for notifications only

	return func() {}
}

// --------------------------------------------------------------------------

func main() {
	cfgPath := "./config.json"   // <-- change to your config location
	callback := func(cfg interface{}) {
		fmt.Println("🔄 Config reloaded →", cfg)
	}
	ReloadConfig(cfgPath, callback) // starts watching automatically

	// Keep the program alive – in a real service you’d run this inside an HTTP server loop.
	select {}
}
```

### How it works (Linux/macOS)

* **`fsnotify.Watch`** (`os.OpenFile(..., os.O_NOTIFY, 0)`) registers interest in file‑modification events.  
* The goroutine reads the whole file and parses JSON with `json.Unmarshal`.  
* A simple timestamp check guarantees we only act when the *content* actually changed.

> **Note:** On Windows you’d use `fsnotify` or wrap it in a small wrapper that falls back to polling (`time.Sleep`). The core idea stays identical: watch for change events, read‑parse‑call callback.

## 🎯 Quick “plug‑and‑play” checklist

| Language | Install (if needed) | Minimal code snippet |
|----------|---------------------|----------------------|
| **TS**   | Node ≥ 14 (`npm i` already has `fs`) | See `reloadConfig` above. |
| **Python** | Standard library – no extra deps. | Call `reload_config('/path/to/config.json', print_cfg)`. |
| **Go**    | Go 1.20+ (standard). No external packages needed. | Run the file; it starts watching immediately. |

All three examples:

* **Never block** the main thread – they run in a background loop or via OS notifications.  
* **No restarts required** – your running process can keep using the freshly‑loaded config while you edit on disk (useful for dev servers, CLI tools, micro‑services).  

Feel free to adapt the callback signature (`(newCfg: T) => void`, `callback(cfg)` or just a plain function) and swap in YAML/INI parsers if your project uses another format. Happy hot‑reloading!
